4 Context Engineering

Lecture slides for this chapter

Purpose

Agent stack focus for this chapter.

Why can an agent lose the thread of a task even when its entire history still fits?

An agent’s context window is a finite and expensive resource, not an unbounded repository for raw text. Naively accumulating tool outputs, conversational turns, and retrieved snippets exhausts token capacity, inflates prefill latency, and spreads attention across material the next decision does not need. Fitting is not the same as being usable. A request that fits can still place a refuted hypothesis beside the test that refuted it, or present a result from an earlier revision as a fact about the current one. Effective context engineering treats each request as a deliberately selected working set, in which relevant state is compacted without losing its provenance, superseded observations are withdrawn, and bulky artifacts stay in durable storage behind handles. Stale or truncated evidence that does reach the model produces errors that compound across later turns. The systems challenge is to carry an accurate, minimal projection of the environment within the token budget, so that the state an agent carries bounds its uncertainty rather than compounding it.

Learning Objectives
  • Distinguish nominal request capacity from usable working context and explain why admitted tokens dilute needed evidence
  • Allocate the next request’s input budget while preserving output headroom and task-relevant evidence
  • Design a context layout that orders entries by change rate for prefix reuse and binds each observation to its evidential scope
  • Compare compaction policies by information loss, recovery requirements, and the cost of repeated transformations
  • Audit a progress record against runtime-owned sources and quantify how unchecked per-round constraint loss compounds across compaction cycles
  • Apply dependency-aware invalidation without treating historical observations as proof of current state
  • Evaluate context policies by decision quality, accepted outcomes, and total resource use under common limits
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

4.1 Carrying a Repair Across Turns

The repair agent (Four lighthouse workloads) has spent several turns investigating a connection-pool defect. It has inspected the pool implementation, executed a reproduction test, and revised its initial diagnosis. Deliberation has left behind candidate versions, a refuted timeout hypothesis, test observations, and the dependency records that tie them together (Revising Candidate Plans). None of that progress lives inside the model. Because foundation models are stateless functions across turns, every call begins and ends with the payload assembled by the host runtime. The agent can use the test trace, honor public API constraints, and avoid revisiting a rejected hypothesis only if the runtime includes them in the upcoming request. The runtime must also structure that request so that the status and role of each artifact remain unambiguous.

Working context governs the within-session state exposure by selecting, representing, and refreshing what each call carries.

The token accounting of Tokenizing the Request settles two questions for an individual request: whether the payload satisfies the admission bound, and what its tokens cost (The Cost of a Model Call). Those bounds, however, do not determine whether the prompt helps the model make a sound decision. A prompt can fit well within token limits and still fail to guide the agent toward a valid repair. The simplest agent architectures evade this problem by treating context as an append-only log, concatenating every observation, tool execution, and partial response into an expanding transcript. That policy breaks down almost immediately. Even while the transcript fits within physical limits, the runtime pays repeatedly for obsolete tokens that the next decision no longer requires. Stale code snippets sit alongside their replacements, and refuted hypotheses linger alongside the test results that disproved them, diluting the model’s attention. When an extended trajectory inevitably exhausts the context window, an unmanaged runtime must truncate blindly, evicting instructions or early constraints without any principled criterion for what to preserve.

↩︎ The quadratic input cost of resending an append-only history on every call is established in How multi-turn history drives up input cost.

The agent’s working context is the runtime subsystem that decides what each model call receives. Rather than treating context as a passive transcript, this subsystem constructs a bounded working set tailored to a single decision. It establishes token budgets for each distinct role in the prompt and arranges data layouts so unchanged prefixes can be reused across turns. It compacts historical evidence without distorting the facts, maintains structured progress records that resist summarization drift, and enforces invalidation rules whenever external actions alter environment state. The host runtime owns assembly, token budgets, records, and execution gates. Selection and compaction can use heuristics or model-generated candidates, which the runtime must check against the task’s requirements and retained evidence before admission.

The chapter follows the repair agent through its next decision, and each section answers the question the previous one leaves open. The runtime first decides which evidence the next step needs and how the input allowance divides among competing roles (section 4.2). Admitting that evidence does not mean the model can use it, so section 4.3 separates the window an interface accepts from the context a model reliably acts on. The selected items then need a layout that keeps their sources distinct and their stable prefix reusable across turns (section 4.4). When history outgrows the budget, compaction (section 4.5) and audited progress records (section 4.6) shorten it without erasing causal evidence or refuted hypotheses. When the agent’s own edits change the workspace, invalidation (section 4.7) withdraws the observations that no longer describe it. Evaluation (section 4.8) then asks how to show that a complete policy lowers the cost per accepted task.

4.2 The Working-Set Decision

As our connection-pool investigation unfolds, the runtime accumulates far more information than was present at initialization. A controlled test has shown that a connection returned to the pool while a waiting client remained blocked, so the initial timeout hypothesis has lost support and missed notification is now the leading candidate explanation. The next model call must build on these findings. If the runtime replays the entire conversational history without distinguishing raw observations from superseded hypotheses, it forces the model to weigh discounted failure modes alongside the evidence that disproved them.

Consider the codebase at version r17, where the relevant files are src/pool.py and tests/test_pool.py, and the failure is captured by the reproducer test_waiter_notified_on_return. Before prompting the model for a candidate patch, the runtime must decide which source excerpts, test results, interface constraints, and open questions belong in the upcoming prompt. This choice is the working-set decision, the runtime’s selection of the bounded subset of task information needed to run the next concrete step. Making that choice is the first obligation of treating context as a selected working set (principle 4).

The term draws on Denning’s classic working-set model of program behavior (Denning 1968). Over any time window, a running program references only a fraction of its total address space, so an operating system avoids thrashing by keeping those active pages in physical memory while leaving dormant pages on backing storage. An agent runtime faces a similar residency problem when it stages tokens in context to support the next step without exhausting its prompt budget, yet one structural difference separates context engineering from virtual memory. Denning’s working set is measured from page references the program has already issued, whereas an agent runtime must assemble the context working set before calling the model, predicting what the upcoming decision will need.1 Moreover, standard foundation model interfaces report only generated tokens and emit no hardware-style reference bits to show which staged context tokens the model actually relied on during inference.

1 Optimal Replacement (MIN): Belady (1966) evaluated page-replacement algorithms against an optimal offline baseline that evicts the page whose next reference lies farthest in the future. Practical operating systems approximate this unattainable ideal using historical access patterns such as least recently used (LRU). An agent context runtime lacks even that access history. The model interface reports no reference bits for the tokens it processes, so working-set selection must rely on explicit domain heuristics and structured task state rather than on observed cache reuse.

Belady, Laszlo A. 1966. “A Study of Replacement Algorithms for a Virtual-Storage Computer.” IBM Systems Journal 5 (2): 78–101. https://doi.org/10.1147/sj.52.0078.

In context management, relevance to the next step must govern selection rather than chronological recency. Core user constraints set at the start of a session remain binding after dozens of intermediate tool calls, whereas a lengthy test log produced moments ago may contribute only a single exit code. Candidate information also comes from well beyond the dialogue history: system instructions, tool declarations, newly read source files, and retrieved reference documents (Lewis et al. 2020) all compete for space in the assembled request. The runtime must build a working representation from these sources without letting any staged copy outrank its origin, because authority stays with the source (principle 6). The environment remains the only authority on system state, and runtime policy, not the model, decides which actions are permitted.

To organize this selection, the runtime builds a context manifest, a structured inventory that lists each item chosen for the upcoming request and why the next step needs it. The manifest for the connection-pool repair’s next request (table 1) reads like a specification rather than a raw byte stream, naming each entry and the exact content it supplies. Code snippets and test logs keep their source and version labels, and their contents enter the prompt only where the next decision requires them.

Building this manifest requires careful filtering. Keeping a failure log without tying it to its source commit (r17) can mislead later turns into reading an old error trace as evidence against a newly patched file. Likewise, recording a short label like “notification bug” without noting its uncertainty turns a working hypothesis into a settled root cause. Conversely, keeping the full dialogue of rejected theories wastes space once the runtime records which test refuted the timeout hypothesis. While a foundation model can help summarize earlier turns to draft this manifest, the runtime must check those candidate summaries against recorded environment observations before admitting them into the prompt.

The active working context must also stay separate from durable records in external storage. Rather than inlining a long test log into the prompt, the runtime can supply a raw-payload handle, a short reference to an external record that an authorized tool can read on demand under access and retention rules. This design enforces the handle rule: once a payload moves behind a handle, it re-enters the working context only through an explicit, checked read. If a later step needs a specific stack-trace line or configuration value omitted from the initial prompt, the model calls a retrieval tool to bring that slice into context.

Every working-set candidate must fit within the endpoint’s context admission bounds. When an endpoint shares one context window across inputs and outputs, the serialized prompt length \(\SequenceLength\) must satisfy \(\SequenceLength \le \ContextWindow - \OutputLimit\), where \(\OutputLimit\) is the token budget reserved for generation. When providers enforce separate input and output limits, \(\SequenceLength \le \ContextWindow\) applies directly. The runtime must count tokens on the fully formatted wire request (system envelopes, chat delimiters, and JSON tool schemas included) rather than summing isolated strings, because tokenizer merges and framing overhead can easily erase the savings of a shorter evidence block.

↩︎ The admission bound and the output headroom \(\OutputLimit\) it reserves are established in Output headroom.

While this inequality sets the overall ceiling, the runtime must divide the available input space across the four roles in the manifest:

\[ \SystemInstructionTokenCount + \ToolDeclarationTokenCount + \TaskStateTokenCount + \ObservationTokenCount \le \ContextWindow - \OutputLimit. \tag{1}\]

Here \(\SystemInstructionTokenCount\) counts the static system instructions, \(\ToolDeclarationTokenCount\) counts API schemas and tool declarations, \(\TaskStateTokenCount\) counts active constraints, progress records, and staged source or test excerpts, and \(\ObservationTokenCount\) counts recent tool outputs and user steering. Because all four terms share a fixed ceiling, growth in any one term squeezes the others. A runtime that appends every raw tool output2 lets \(\ObservationTokenCount\) grow on every turn until command-line logs crowd out \(\TaskStateTokenCount\), the constraints and code excerpts the next decision needs. Whereas appending lets observations expand until they displace task state, selection holds each role’s share steady by moving older results into external records that a handle can still reach (figure 1).

2 Interleaved Observations: ReAct prompting interleaves reasoning traces, tool actions, and observation payloads within an ever-expanding prompt transcript (Yao et al. 2023). While keeping full execution history visible simplifies early prototyping, it carries a steep cost, because the unchecked growth of \(\ObservationTokenCount\) consumes prompt capacity and raises input token costs on every subsequent call.

Yao, Shunyu, Jeffrey Zhao, Dian Yu, Nan Du, Izhak Shafran, Karthik Narasimhan, and Yuan Cao. 2023. “ReAct: Synergizing Reasoning and Acting in Language Models.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).

Equation 1 is a planning budget rather than an exact identity, because sub-word tokenization across joined strings does not equal the sum of separately tokenized parts. The runtime must therefore check the assembled wire request against the provider’s limit, and when the request runs over, the budget partition tells the runtime which share (usually the observation buffer or older history) to trim or compact first. Prompt staging (section 4.4) then orders these same four components by how often they change so the serving engine can reuse their cached prefix.

Napkin Math 4.1: How much of the window is evidence
Problem: In our reference repository-repair harness, an agent runs inside a 128,000-token context window under a 30-turn step budget. The runtime reserves 8,000 tokens for generation (\(\OutputLimit\)) and assigns 3,000 tokens to system instructions (\(\SystemInstructionTokenCount\)) and 12,000 tokens to tool declarations (\(\ToolDeclarationTokenCount\)). Dynamic task state (constraints, progress records, and active code excerpts) takes 24,000 tokens (\(\TaskStateTokenCount\)). If every raw tool result is appended directly at 4,000 tokens per turn, how many turns fit within the window, and what maximum per-turn observation size lets the agent finish the full step budget?

Variables: The context window and step horizon come from the registry’s repository-repair harness profile, while component sizes and per-turn observation counts are scenario values for fully serialized wire requests.

Math: By equation 1, the usable input allowance is 128,000 − 8,000 = 120,000 tokens. Subtracting system instructions and tool declarations leaves 105,000 tokens (82.0 percent of the context window) for state and observations, and deducting the 24,000 tokens of state leaves an observation budget of 81,000 tokens. Under naive appending, this budget holds 81,000 / 4,000 = 20 turns. Lasting all 30 turns requires keeping the per-turn observation payload to at most 81,000 / 30 = 2,700 tokens.

Result: The appending policy overflows the context window at turn 21, well short of the 30-turn budget.

Systems insight: Because the number of feasible turns equals the observation budget divided by the per-turn observation size, observation volume is the primary design lever. Compacting each result into an informative 2,000-token digest extends the run to 40 turns and clears the step budget. Fitting inside the window, however, is only the first cost. Because an appending policy re-reads every accumulated token on each call, its cumulative input bill grows quadratically in \(\TurnHorizon\). This follows the re-send pattern of equation 2.9 with a per-turn append \(\TurnAppendLength\) of 4,000 tokens, where the first request already holds one tool result, so the appended sum runs to \(\TurnHorizon(\TurnHorizon+1)/2\). Selection keeps the bill linear. Across this step budget, and even granting the appending policy a window large enough to admit its final request, appending bills 3,030,000 input tokens against 1,410,000 for selection, 2.1× as many. That extra spend enters the numerator of \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\) (equation 1.2) on every attempt.

Appending re-reads every result, so its bill bends upward; selection stays linear.

The notebooks throughout this chapter reuse this baseline scenario, adopting the 128,000-token window, the 30-turn step budget, and the component token allocations defined above. Whereas the repair agent binds on the growth of \(\ObservationTokenCount\) against \(\TaskStateTokenCount\), the other three lighthouses stress different terms of the same partition.

Lighthouse 4.1: Context by archetype

Each recurring workload places pressure on a distinct component of the context budget in equation 1, calling for a different runtime mechanism (table 2).

Systems insight: The same budget equation binds on different constraints across workloads: token growth for the repair agent, source tracking for the research agent, freshness for the remediation agent, and prefix reuse across short sessions for the support agent. As a result, a policy tuned for one workload rarely transfers directly to another. Compaction rules that keep a repair agent within its token budget do not solve the main risk of a research agent, where untrusted external text matters far more than transcript length.

Every one of these budgets governs admission, ensuring that a serialized payload fits inside the provider’s context limit, yet admission alone does not mean the prompt supports the next decision. A prompt can obey the budget while omitting the return path needed to write a patch, or it can include every relevant code excerpt while burying version boundaries under old error logs. Sizing a context budget is therefore only the first step; the runtime must also ask how well the model can find and use the tokens admitted into that window.

4.3 Usable Working Context

In notebook 4.1, the 128,000-token window admits the investigation’s full transcript for its first 20 turns, yet fitting an entire history inside the window does not mean the runtime should send all of it. What matters is what each item contributes to the pending decision: an omitted synchronization contract can ruin a proposed repair, whereas ten pages of repeated timeout speculation only add noise. Raw request length cannot separate essential evidence from distraction.

The nominal context capacity is the maximum sequence length that the configured model interface accepts under its input and generation limits. How well the model reasons over a chosen set of instructions and evidence is a separate question, answered only by testing concrete tasks against explicit quality criteria (principle 4).

Definition 4.1: Usable working context

Usable working context is the context content, organization, and length with which a specified model and invocation configuration meet a chosen quality criterion on a stated workload.

  1. Significance: Usable working context, rather than the nominal window, determines how much of the input allowance \(\ContextWindow-\OutputLimit\) effectively supports decisions. It dictates how many turns an execution trajectory can sustain before selection or compaction must intervene, directly setting the input token cost per accepted task in \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\).
  2. Distinction: Nominal capacity is an architectural boundary of the serving interface that admits a request; usable working context is an empirical property of a task, model, and representation that only evaluation can establish.
  3. Common pitfall: Treating a fixed fraction of the nominal window, or success on an isolated synthetic retrieval probe, as a universal guarantee of usable context for a production workload.

For a software repair workload, that criterion might require candidate patches to pass designated regression tests while staying within a permitted edit scope, whereas an information extraction workload requires high precision and recall against a target schema. Because failure modes differ across workloads, usable working context must be measured on each workload rather than read off the model card.

4.3.1 Testing usable working context

Testing whether a context length is usable starts with what the task asks the model to do with the staged text. A model that scores nearly perfectly on an isolated retrieval probe3 by finding a single planted fact in a long input can still fail a repair decision at the same length. The repair requires relating observed symptoms to source files, honoring API invariants, and separating a measured failure from an unverified hypothesis (table 3). Synthetic retrieval probes quickly flag gross blind spots, but validating a context policy requires testing actual task decisions under realistic conditions.

3 Needle-in-a-Haystack Probes: The best-known isolated retrieval probe plants a short fact, the needle, in long distractor text and asks the model to return it. Hsieh et al. (2024) found that models scoring nearly perfectly on this probe degraded sharply as length grew on tasks that require multi-hop tracing and aggregation, and that only about half of the models tested held satisfactory performance at the context length all of them claimed to support. The gap between the probe and the harder tasks is the gap between nominal capacity and usable working context.

Hsieh, Cheng-Ping, Simeng Sun, Samuel Kriman, Shantanu Acharya, Dima Rekesh, Fei Jia, Yang Zhang, and Boris Ginsburg. 2024. “RULER: What’s the Real Context Size of Your Long-Context Language Models?” Conference on Language Modeling (COLM).

Context organization directly affects task success even when all required facts are present in the prompt. Evaluating multi-document question answering and key-value retrieval, Liu et al. (2024) reported marked sensitivity to the position of relevant information across model architectures, with accuracy dropping substantially when key facts appeared in the middle of the input. This position sensitivity, known as lost in the middle, means systems engineers cannot treat context as an unordered bag of tokens. Placement policies must be tested on the target workload, because how accuracy varies with position depends on the model family and prompt layout.

Length degrades comprehension even when every required fact remains in the input. One long-context benchmark isolates that effect by rewriting each of its stories at lengths from a few hundred tokens to the full text while preserving the details its questions depend on. A change in score across lengths therefore reflects the surrounding material rather than missing evidence (Fiction.live 2025). Answering requires following the order of events and drawing inferences from implicitly stated information, a task closer to a repair decision than to returning a planted value. Figure 2 plots the scores against the length of story in context, as aggregated by Epoch AI (2026).

Two features of figure 2 bear directly on the working budget: the decline starts early and runs deep, and models differ widely at any fixed length. Across the 53 fully scored models, the median falls from 100 percent on the shortest version to 58 percent at 8K tokens and 56 percent at 60K, with only 3 models staying at or above 90 percent at 60K. At 60K tokens, scores also span from 22 to 100 percent. This spread is the empirical content of the definition above. Usable working context belongs to a specified model, configuration, and workload, so the budget must be found by evaluation rather than read from the window.

A first-order mechanism explains why appending distractor tokens cannot be treated as free. Within an individual attention head, a query assigns weight to each visible key through a softmax operation (Vaswani et al. 2017). For a target key with scaled logit \(\TargetKeyLogit\) alongside \(\CompetingKeyCount\) other keys with logits \(\CompetingKeyLogit_j = \QueryVector\cdot\KeyVector_j/\sqrt{\AttentionHeadDimension}\) for head dimension \(\AttentionHeadDimension\), the target’s weight is

\[ \TargetKeyAttentionWeight = \frac{e^{\TargetKeyLogit}}{e^{\TargetKeyLogit} + \sum_{j=1}^{\CompetingKeyCount} e^{\CompetingKeyLogit_j}}. \tag{2}\]

Fixed-logit distractors shrink the target’s share of one normalizer.

All weights share a single normalizer. Because each additional key contributes a strictly positive term \(e^{\CompetingKeyLogit_j}\) to the denominator of equation 2, expanding the sequence length while holding logits constant depresses the target key’s share of attention. This structural decay is attention dilution. In a toy head with a target logit of 6 and every background logit at -2, the target commands 96.8 percent of the weight among 100 keys. When the context expands to 100,000 keys, that same target’s share falls to 2.9 percent, even though no single background key is individually competitive.

In agent execution, this effect is often worse than unrelated filler would produce.4 Stale tool outputs, superseded logs, and earlier source reads share identifiers such as waiter, connection, and timeout with the evidence that matters, so they produce large inner products with query vectors and keep elevated logits. This dynamic is a sufficient condition for dilution under fixed logits, not a deterministic forecast of failure. Production models sharpen logits, route evidence across multiple attention heads and layers, and learn position-dependent retrieval behaviors, which is why Liu et al. (2024) measured degradation empirically rather than deriving it analytically. Trained models also show a second consequence of the shared normalizer. Because the weights must sum to one, a query with no strong match deposits its leftover weight on the first few positions, which act as attention sinks regardless of their content (Xiao et al. 2024). Sinks change where leftover weight goes, not the dilution argument, and every staged token still adds a term to the normalizer that the evidence for the next decision must compete against.

4 Distracting Context: Shi et al. (2023) added irrelevant sentences to grade-school arithmetic problems and found that model accuracy fell dramatically, although the added text changed nothing about the answer. Because the distractors were irrelevant by construction, the drop measures interference rather than missing information. That interference is the cost an agent pays for every superseded log line and stale file read it keeps in the request.

Shi, Freda, Xinyun Chen, Kanishka Misra, Nathan Scales, David Dohan, Ed H. Chi, Nathanael Schärli, and Denny Zhou. 2023. “Large Language Models Can Be Easily Distracted by Irrelevant Context.” International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML), Proceedings of machine learning research, vol. 202: 31210–27.
Xiao, Guangxuan, Yuandong Tian, Beidi Chen, Song Han, and Mike Lewis. 2024. “Efficient Streaming Language Models with Attention Sinks.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).

↪ Why cache eviction policies must retain attention-sink tokens is developed in Reclamation vs. approximation.

4.3.2 Choosing a working budget

Sizing a working budget from the window’s admission bound alone can still leave a request too long for the model to use reliably. Choosing a working budget therefore takes two steps, and only the first can be computed from the window. In notebook 4.1, the admission bound leaves 81,000 tokens for observations in any one request. An appending policy must hold each result to 2,700 tokens to last all 30 turns, whereas a selecting policy can spend up to that whole share on every request because it does not accumulate. The ceiling says what fits, not what the next decision can use, so the second step searches beneath it empirically.

To find that working point, engineers test complete context policies on representative task suites (section 4.8) and choose the budget that meets the workload’s quality criterion at the lowest effective cost. Because a context policy sets a token limit alongside what material is retrieved, how structured data is formatted, and where each piece sits in the prompt, decision quality must be weighed alongside input size, latency, and token spend. Additional context can supply needed grounding, add harmless noise, or degrade reasoning through dilution, which means the quality curve is rarely monotonic and seldom has a single sharp peak. A documented operating envelope must therefore name the workload, model configuration, quality threshold, and test conditions.

These choices carry direct resource consequences under model-call cost accounting. Uncached tokens add prefill latency and compute charges, whereas aligned prefix caching spreads prompt costs across repeated turns. High cache hit rates or discounted per-token prices mean little if stale context hurts decisions, yet pruning context too sharply to save prefill cost can strip away required constraints. The engineering goal is to meet the task quality bar at the lowest effective cost per accepted task, \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\) (equation 1.2). Trimming unneeded tokens lowers the attempt cost in the numerator, whereas dropping a needed fact lowers the acceptance probability \(\AcceptanceProbability\) in the denominator and drives up total spend across failed runs.

In the connection-pool repair example, keeping the full transcript of early exploratory turns wastes space once distilled test observations, code snippets, interface invariants, and active hypotheses are ready to guide the next patch. Stripping away a required interface contract or error log merely to fit an arbitrary sub-budget, however, is just as harmful. When no candidate policy meets both the task criterion and the available budget, the runtime must fetch the missing information, narrow the scope of the next permitted action, or report the limit. Passing a request-admission check never certifies that the staged context is adequate. Once the runtime selects that working set and bounds its token budget, it must arrange those items into a concrete prompt layout that keeps sources and evidence relationships distinct while separating stable prefix content from turn-by-turn updates.

4.4 Staging the Next Invocation

In our connection-pool repair task, the selected manifest holds several distinct kinds of material: public API constraints, source excerpts from src/pool.py at revision r17, test results from test_waiter_notified_on_return, and the missed-notification working hypothesis alongside an abandoned timeout explanation. Concatenating these items into a flat, unlabeled string would blur the distinctions that guide the model’s next decision. Once a selection policy chooses the working set for an upcoming call, staging turns those entries into a structured request payload. That assembly step records the source, authority, and revision of every item so the model can tell policy instructions apart from test observations and working hypotheses, while ordering the content to minimize token churn across consecutive calls.

4.4.1 Ordering by change rate

Minimizing token churn across calls means ordering the prompt by how often each piece of context changes, placing stable material ahead of volatile updates. This layout partitions the serialized prompt into three regions: the root, the trunk, and the leaf, which group content strictly by its update cadence during a trajectory rather than by message role or authority (table 4). In the repair scenario from notebook 4.1, the root holds 3,000 tokens of system instructions and 12,000 tokens of tool declarations. The trunk holds 24,000 tokens of evolving task state, and the leaf receives 8,000 tokens of newly staged observations.

Prefix stability must never override correctness. The root stays fixed only while its declarations remain accurate; if a tool definition changes during a session, the runtime updates the root on the next call and accepts the cache miss. Likewise, when a candidate patch advances the repository from r17 to r18, the runtime cannot keep r17 source code in the trunk just to keep the prefix warm. The earlier test failure remains valid historical evidence about r17, but checking whether it persists on the patched workspace requires a fresh run (section 4.7).

Information can also move between zones as a trajectory advances. An observation first staged in the leaf can fold into the trunk’s progress record during a scheduled refresh so long as its source, version, and scope stay attached. Conversely, a per-step instruction injected into the leaf keeps its full authority despite sitting at the end of the prompt. Zone placement sets the physical order of tokens to match their change rate, whereas schemas and message roles carry provenance and meaning.

4.4.2 Preserving source and scope

As observations move from the leaf into the trunk or across compaction passes, each entry must keep the revision and execution conditions that produced it so the model does not read a local result as a universal fact about the codebase. For our failing test case, the host runtime attaches the test name, the workspace revision (r17), the test configuration, and a run identifier directly to the observation payload. This discipline enforces the scope rule:5 an observation supports claims only for the artifact versions and execution conditions under which it was gathered, and every later context transformation (summarization, compaction, and invalidation alike) must preserve those bounds.

5 Why- and Where-Provenance: Database research distinguishes why-provenance, the source records that justify a result’s existence, from where-provenance, the source locations its values were copied from (Buneman et al. 2001). The scope rule demands both for every observation: which execution and artifact versions justify its existence, and which payload spans supplied its values. A result that loses either becomes an unattributed claim that later compaction can silently generalize.

Buneman, Peter, Sanjeev Khanna, and Wang-Chiew Tan. 2001. “Why and Where: A Characterization of Data Provenance.” Database Theory – ICDT 2001, Lecture notes in computer science, vol. 1973: 316–30. https://doi.org/10.1007/3-540-44503-X_20.
Greshake, Kai, Sahar Abdelnabi, Shailesh Mishra, Christoph Endres, Thorsten Holz, and Mario Fritz. 2023. “Not What You’ve Signed up for: Compromising Real-World LLM-Integrated Applications with Indirect Prompt Injection.” Proceedings of the 16th ACM Workshop on Artificial Intelligence and Security, 79–90. https://doi.org/10.1145/3605764.3623985.

The runtime builds this framing on the outside rather than reading it from text inside the observation. A serializer places raw tool output strictly as a literal data value inside a structured envelope so that mismatched quotes, markdown delimiters, or control characters cannot corrupt the surrounding schema. Even with that framing, the payload itself remains an untrusted span: arbitrary text whose arrival the runtime can record, but whose claims it cannot vouch for. In a traditional parser, escaping quotes keeps a string literal out of the executable syntax tree. A language model, by contrast, processes the framing tags and the escaped data string inside them as one sequence of tokens in the same attention pass. Careful encoding therefore stops format injection at the host serializer, yet it cannot stop the model from attending to and following adversarial instructions hidden inside the text (Greshake et al. 2023).

Provenance labels therefore guide interpretation rather than enforcing security. Marking a tool output as untrusted data helps the model weigh its source, but if an observation tells the agent to drop a database or overwrite production credentials, the model may still propose that call. Security boundaries (filesystem sandboxes, read-only mounts, and deployment blocks) must run in host code outside the model’s prompt interpretation: staging keeps instructions and observations distinct in the request, while host gates enforce what may run.

↪ The host-side information-flow policy that limits what untrusted context may influence and where private data may flow is enforced in Information-Flow Policy.

How much weight this distinction carries depends on the workload. The repair agent’s observations come mostly from its own workspace, whose files change only when the agent or its tests change them. The research agent fills its requests with third-party text, so its \(\ObservationTokenCount\) consists mostly of untrusted spans (lighthouse 4.1). For that workload the untrusted-span boundary, not token growth, is the binding concern. Once each entry’s source and scope are framed, the serializer must emit the root and trunk in a repeatable token sequence so the serving engine can reuse their cached attention state.

4.4.3 Preserving an exact prefix

Because causal self-attention computes each token’s keys and values from all preceding tokens, a single changed token near the beginning of a request invalidates the cached key-value (KV) attention state for every later position, even when thousands of trailing tokens are identical. Serving engines equipped with prefix caching6 skip redundant prefill computation only across an identical leading token sequence, because attention state is derived from the token prefix (principle 5). Ordering the zones by change rate is what turns this rule into savings. A call that changes only the leaf reuses the root and trunk, whereas a compaction that rewrites the trunk ends reuse at the root boundary and forces recomputation of every later token (figure 3).

6 Automatic Prefix Reuse: Serving systems such as SGLang index cached attention state by token prefix within a radix tree, allowing an incoming request that shares an existing token prefix to reuse cached KV blocks transparently (Zheng et al. 2024). Because radix tree lookups evaluate exact token equality rather than semantic equivalence, two requests that diverge on an early timestamp share zero cache entries past that offset. Layout discipline directly determines cache hit rates.

Zheng, Lianmin, Liangsheng Ying, Zhenghao Yin, Cody Hao Liu, Eric Yu, Prakhar Patel, Zhipeng Yang, Anthony K. Joseph, and Ion Stoica. 2024. “SGLang: Efficient Execution of Structured Language Model Programs.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2312.07104, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2312.07104.

Cache equality depends on the final serialized token sequence rather than on high-level application state. Small serialization changes (JSON key order, whitespace padding, prompt template edits, or token merges across boundaries) can quietly alter the token stream. For example, byte-pair encodings (BPE) often merge trailing whitespace with the leading characters of the next string, producing different token IDs at zone joins. Prefix stability must therefore be checked on the assembled, tokenized wire request.

An identical token prefix is necessary for cache reuse, but it is not sufficient on its own. The serving engine must also still hold compatible cache blocks in accelerator memory under the same model weights, positional encoding offsets, and attention configuration. Memory pressure, LRU eviction, or cluster autoscaling can drop cached blocks between turns even when the request prefix is byte-for-byte identical. While the serving engine manages and evicts those internal tensors, the agent runtime is responsible for keeping static material in a repeatable prefix and refreshing whatever state the next decision requires.

↪ Prefix compatibility and the retention and eviction of cached KV blocks in the serving engine are developed in Prefix Caching Across Turns.

Placing fast-changing metadata near the start of a request forfeits prefix reuse for no benefit, whereas moving per-call fields (request IDs, wall-clock timestamps, step counters) into the leaf preserves reuse across the root and trunk. Whenever an early field changes, cache sharing stops at the first differing token. Consider the extreme case where the first token changes on every call, which bounds the extra cost of putting volatile fields at the front of a request.

Napkin Math 4.2: Pricing a context layout across one trajectory
Problem: The repair agent of notebook 4.1 runs its full 30-turn step budget under the selecting policy, making one call per turn. Each request carries a 15,000-token root of instructions and tool declarations, a 24,000-token trunk of task state, and an 8,000-token leaf of selected observations, measured after serialization. The leaf is replaced on each call rather than appended, and the trunk is rewritten on 2 calls after the first, one for each compaction pass that notebook 4.3 finds this step budget triggers. Input tokens cost a hypothetical $3 per million tokens, with cached reads billed at 10 percent of that price. What input bill follows from each layout?

Variables: Both layouts send 47,000 tokens per request and 1,410,000 input tokens across the trajectory, matching the selecting policy budget from notebook 4.1. In the unstable layout, the first token changes on every call and no prefix is reused. In the stable layout, the first call is cold, the 2 trunk rewrites first differ right after the root, and the remaining 27 calls first differ right after the trunk, with compatible cached state kept for every matching prefix. This calculation isolates input token pricing, omitting generation charges and cache storage fees.

Math: The unstable layout processes all input without reuse, billing $4.23. In the stable layout, the initial cold call processes the whole request, each trunk rewrite prefills the trunk and leaf, and every warm call processes only the leaf, for a total uncached volume of \(47{,}000 + 2 \times 32{,}000 + 27 \times 8{,}000 = 327{,}000\) tokens. The remaining 1,083,000 tokens hit the prefix cache (76.8 percent of all input tokens). Billing uncached tokens at full price and reused tokens at the discounted rate gives $1.31 for the trajectory.

Result: Average input cost drops from $0.141 per call in the unstable layout to $0.044 in the stable layout. An unstable layout bills 3.2× the stable layout and requires 4.3× more uncached prefill tokens. These figures are billing and token-volume ratios, not end-to-end execution speedups.

Systems insight: With a stable prefix, the size of the volatile leaf sets the marginal input cost of each warm call, whereas rewriting the trunk prefills 32,000 tokens, the cost of 4 leaf-only calls. Engineers should keep per-call dynamic fields in the leaf and rewrite the trunk only when workspace files or active task constraints change. That discipline lowers the input share of attempt cost in \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\) by the ratio above, while keeping task accuracy (\(\AcceptanceProbability\)) intact because the staged context still reflects current environment state. A high cache hit rate never justifies keeping stale context.

Checkpoint 4.1: Checking the staged request

A candidate patch has just moved the workspace from r17 to r18, and the runtime is staging the next request.

Staging puts the selected evidence into a clear schema and an order built for prefix reuse, yet ordering alone does not shrink long tool outputs: an oversized test log or large source file still takes its full share of the budget. When the needed working set outgrows the available budget, the runtime must shorten the representation itself while keeping the evidence later decisions need.

4.5 Context Compaction

As the connection-pool repair runs across turns, it accumulates repeated source listings, boilerplate test-setup output, and long execution traces. Keeping every line in every prompt uses space that later turns need for patch inspection and regression checks. Yet collapsing that trace into a bare note like “the test failed” drops the clues that matter: which revision failed, which thread schedule ran, and which assertion tripped. Compaction must shorten the working context without erasing the causal links future decisions rely on.

Definition 4.2: Context compaction

Context compaction replaces material in a working context with a shorter representation chosen for the task’s next decisions.

  1. Significance: It creates room for new observations inside the fixed input allowance \(\ContextWindow-\OutputLimit\), allowing an agent trajectory to run past the turn at which appending would overflow while retaining critical evidence, constraints, and unresolved obligations.
  2. Distinction: A compressed representation is inherently lossy or access-deferred. Keeping a handle to omitted material makes recovery conditional on an explicit retrieval step.
  3. Common pitfall: Assuming that a deterministic extractor, or a summary mentioning all required field names, guarantees that no decision-critical information was lost.

4.5.1 Choosing a compaction method

A regular-expression filter trims log lines deterministically, but it can drop the one line that explains an intermittent fault. A source-code outline keeps API signatures while stripping the locking logic needed to spot a missed wakeup, and an LLM-written summary can preserve high-level diagnostic intent while inventing details or misstating whether a check passed.7 Different compaction methods thus trade token savings against validation work and the risk of silent information loss in different ways, and no single method fits all runtime data.

7 Unfaithful Summaries: In a human evaluation of neural abstractive summarizers, Maynez et al. (2020) found hallucinated content (statements unsupported by the source document) in the output of every model studied. A progress record written by a model inherits this failure mode. The record audit of section 4.6.2 therefore compares each claimed milestone with its recorded event instead of trusting the summarizer’s account.

Maynez, Joshua, Shashi Narayan, Bernd Bohnet, and Ryan McDonald. 2020. “On Faithfulness and Factuality in Abstractive Summarization.” Proceedings of the 58th Annual Meeting of the Association for Computational Linguistics (ACL), 1906–19. https://doi.org/10.18653/v1/2020.acl-main.173.

The right representation depends on the next step: checking an interface takes only an outline, whereas debugging a race condition requires full function bodies and thread-interleaving logs. Each option in table 5 is therefore defined by what it keeps in the request and what the runtime must check, rather than by a fixed reduction ratio.

The first three classes in table 5 use deterministic rules that run in host code without calling a language model. Table 6 breaks those three rows into the concrete techniques a runtime builds and asks what specific evidence each one can drop without raising an error. Deterministic filtering guarantees repeatable output, but it cannot guarantee that the remaining lines suffice for the task; a filter is safe only when later decisions do not depend on what it cut.

Lossless byte compression offers no shortcut for fitting a large payload into the prompt. Decompressing the bytes before inference still spends the full uncompressed token count against the window, whereas passing compressed bytes or encoded text directly to the tokenizer splits high-entropy byte streams into fine-grained tokens that the model cannot interpret as source code or logs. The alternative that loses nothing is the handle rule from the working-set decision, which pairs a digest in the request with a handle to the retained payload. The raw payload stays in an external artifact store, and the cost of that choice falls on the later step that needs the payload, which pays a read’s latency and the tokens of whatever it brings back. In our connection-pool repair task, running the controlled regression test at revision r17 produces the log excerpt in listing 1 (with repeated polling lines already stripped).

A simple filter that keeps only lines matching FAILED captures the final assertion failure while dropping the event sequence that explains it. Without seeing that waiter_registered_before_return and connection_available_after_return both evaluated to true, the model cannot tell whether the failure came from a registration race, an exhausted pool, or a missed notification. A compact, structured digest keeps that causal chain intact: “At r17, test_waiter_notified_on_return registered the waiter before returning a connection. The connection became available, but assert waiter_resumed_at_notification_check failed at the notification check.” Alongside this digest, the context keeps the revision tags (src/pool.py@r17, tests/test_pool.py@r17), the test schedule, and a handle to the full trace for on-demand inspection.

This short record supports the missed-notification hypothesis under the tested conditions while keeping measured facts separate from diagnostic guesses. When a candidate patch produces revision r18, the runtime records the new test outcome against r18 rather than overwriting or merging it with the r17 failure. Collapsing both runs into an unqualified note such as “the bug is fixed” would erase the provenance needed to check what the patch actually changed.

4.5.2 Protecting task-scoped fields

Keeping that provenance intact across compaction passes requires naming which fields a pass is never allowed to drop. In the connection-pool trace, losing the source revision (pool.py@r17), the test name (test_waiter_notified_on_return), or the concurrency schedule leaves the next turn without the conditions needed to reproduce the fault. The runtime therefore defines a set of protected fields that must survive every compaction transform, and checks before committing an updated working context that those fields remain present and unchanged. Alongside test identity, revision, scheduling flags, failed assertion, and exit status, global task constraints stay pinned as well: preserving the public API, keeping edits inside the approved repository directory, and blocking production deploys. Specific function bodies or configuration lines omitted from the digest can then be paged back in through handles when a later step needs them.

Which fields need protection depends on the current phase of the task. Pinning every historical line number, diff hunk, and identifier defeats compaction by filling the budget, whereas a rigid boilerplate schema can drop the exact relationship a task hinges on, such as an assertion message detached from its commit hash or concurrency schedule. At the same time, runtime filters must scrub credentials, tokens, and private keys so that secret strings are never kept merely because they matched a literal-retention rule.

Protected fields are not limited to static parameters known at the start of a run, because agents often uncover new conditions during execution. Suppose our concurrency reproducer fails reliably under the controlled waiter-before-return schedule but passes most of the time under the default operating-system thread scheduler. That dependency does not appear in the initial task prompt; it emerges only in tool output. A summarizer that records “the waiter test failed” while dropping the schedule flag lets a later turn rerun the test under the default scheduler, see a chance pass on broken code, and declare the repair done. Whenever tool output shows that an outcome depends on a run parameter, the runtime promotes that parameter into the protected-field set alongside the observation it qualifies, subjecting it to the same checks as the original contract constraints.

Automated checks catch missing test names, altered literals, or results attached to the wrong revision, though schema checks alone cannot prove that the inventory covers every needed fact or that the model will use what survives (section 4.8). Before evicting any raw payload, the runtime records what happened to the omitted material: either it is no longer needed, it stays reachable behind a handle, or it has been permanently dropped and leaves an explicit gap in the context. If a later step needs an evicted detail and retrieval fails, the agent must rerun the tool or report the gap rather than making up plausible history.

4.5.3 Scheduling compaction within the request budget

Because each compaction pass folds new leaf observations into the trunk’s progress record, compacting after every small tool output would rewrite the trunk on every turn and forfeit prefix reuse across the trajectory. To avoid paying that trunk-rewrite cost on every step, runtimes separate the trigger threshold from the post-compaction target length. This two-threshold rule, hysteresis,8 lets observations build up in the leaf over several turns before the runtime rewrites the trunk. A narrow gap between the two watermarks causes frequent, costly rewrites, whereas a wider gap lets the agent run longer between passes at the cost of deeper compression when a pass finally triggers. Sizing the gap balances token headroom against information loss: compressing too far risks stripping needed detail, while compressing too little causes thrashing.

8 Watermark Pairs: Operating-system page reclaim uses the same two-threshold pattern. A reclaim thread wakes when free memory falls below a low watermark and stops only after restoring a higher one, allowing reclamation to run in batches rather than on every allocation. The gap balances batch size against invocation frequency, a trade-off that equation 3 quantifies for context compaction.

Let \(\CompactionTriggerLength\) and \(\CompactionTargetLength\) denote the upper trigger threshold and the post-compaction target, measured in total serialized input tokens. Because the runtime must keep room for generation, these watermarks obey \(\CompactionTargetLength < \CompactionTriggerLength \le \ContextWindow - \OutputLimit\), where \(\ContextWindow\) is the context window limit and \(\OutputLimit\) is the reserved output allowance. Both thresholds are runtime policy choices rather than physical model limits.

In an idealized cycle where each compaction pass resets the request to \(\CompactionTargetLength\) and each turn adds \(\TurnAppendLength > 0\) net input tokens, the runtime checks the request length after each addition before calling the model. The integer number of turns between compaction passes is

\[ \CompactionCycleTurnCount = \left\lceil \frac{\CompactionTriggerLength - \CompactionTargetLength}{\TurnAppendLength} \right\rceil. \tag{3}\]

Hysteresis turns compaction into a sawtooth whose gap sets the cadence.

Because context length is checked at turn boundaries, the serialized length just before compaction is \(\CompactionTargetLength + \CompactionCycleTurnCount\TurnAppendLength\), which can overshoot \(\CompactionTriggerLength\) by up to \(\TurnAppendLength - 1\) tokens. That possible overshoot makes \(\CompactionTriggerLength\) a soft threshold: when tool outputs vary widely in size, the runtime must either trim oversized observations or stage them behind handles so a single large output cannot cross the hard ceiling \(\ContextWindow - \OutputLimit\). If the runtime calls a model to write the summary, that compaction call also needs its own input and output budgets.

Compaction directly affects prompt-cache spend. A pass that rewrites the trunk invalidates cached KV blocks from the end of the root onward, incurring the trunk-prefill cost shown in Call \(\Glyph{n}+2\) of figure 3. Over a cycle of \(\CompactionCycleTurnCount\) task turns and one compaction pass, let \({\TaskTurnCost}_{\CompactionCycleTurnIndex}\) be the total cost of task turn \(\CompactionCycleTurnIndex\) (cached and uncached input tokens plus generated output tokens). Let \(\CompactionPassCost\) be the full cost of the compaction pass under the same billing rules. The amortized cost per task turn is

\[ \AmortizedTurnCost = \frac{\sum_{i=1}^{\CompactionCycleTurnCount} {\TaskTurnCost}_{i} + \CompactionPassCost}{\CompactionCycleTurnCount}. \tag{4}\]

Any cache misses caused by rewriting the trunk already appear in the uncached token charges of the following \({\TaskTurnCost}_{\CompactionCycleTurnIndex}\) terms, so adding a separate cache-invalidation penalty would double-count them. Beyond prompt tokens, a full cost comparison must also count external storage, handle-retrieval latency, repeated checks, and runs that fail because needed context was dropped too early. A schedule that minimizes per-turn token spend \(\AmortizedTurnCost\) can still raise the effective cost per accepted task (\(\CostPerAcceptedTask\)) if heavy compression sends the agent down dead ends. The watermark gap \((\CompactionTriggerLength - \CompactionTargetLength)\) sets \(\CompactionCycleTurnCount\) and therefore how many turns share \(\CompactionPassCost\).

Napkin Math 4.3: Spacing compaction passes
Problem: The repair agent from notebook 4.1 appends each 4,000-token tool result and compacts instead of overflowing. Its 128,000-token context window, minus the 8,000-token output reserve, leaves 120,000 tokens for serialized input. Each compaction pass resets the prompt back to 47,000 tokens (matching the baseline selecting policy’s request size), and the next pass triggers at 92,000 tokens. How often does compaction run, and how many passes does the agent execute across its 30-turn step budget?

Variables: Every compaction pass resets the prompt to the target \(\CompactionTargetLength\), and per-turn additions \(\TurnAppendLength\) stay constant at 4,000 tokens. The runtime checks request length after each tool addition before calling the model. The watermarks are runtime policy settings rather than hardware limits.

Math: Applying equation 3 gives \(\CompactionCycleTurnCount = \lceil (92{,}000 - 47{,}000) / 4{,}000 \rceil = 12\) additions between compaction passes. The request reaches the trigger at 95,000 input tokens, overshooting \(\CompactionTriggerLength\) by 3,000 tokens, and adding the output reserve brings the total to 103,000 tokens, still inside the window before compaction runs. Across the 30-turn step budget, the runtime runs 2 compaction passes, each rewriting the trunk. Those are the two trunk rewrites priced in notebook 4.2, where the leaf was replaced on every call rather than growing between passes. Raising the post-compaction target to 81,000 tokens under the same arrival rate shrinks the interval to 3 additions.

Systems insight: Compaction frequency grows with the token arrival rate divided by the watermark gap, so narrowing the gap from 12 arrivals to 3 triggers compaction 4× as often and raises the per-turn compaction overhead in equation 4 by the same factor. Setting a gap smaller than a single turn’s arrival causes thrashing by compacting on every step. Designers should size the gap to span several arrivals and compress toward \(\CompactionTargetLength\) only as far as protected-field rules allow, testing on real tasks where the line between needed context and removable detail lies.

A shortened observation captures only one piece of the repair’s state; finishing the task also requires carrying forward the current candidate patch, the checks already run on it, the remaining test obligations, and the next bounded step. Because free-form prose summaries easily blur those links during compaction, the runtime keeps them in a structured progress record.

4.6 Structured Progress Records

By the time the candidate patch is tested, the repair has moved through three linked events: at r17, the controlled test failed; the agent’s patch then wrote r18; and the reproducer passed on r18. All three events must survive the next compaction with their relationships intact. A naive summary that says only “fixed the notification bug” drops the distinctions the agent needs to choose its next step. The r17 test saw a returned connection leave a waiting thread blocked. The r18 run showed only that the waiter wakes under that single test schedule, which proves nothing yet about the broader regression suite or public API compatibility. Once raw diagnostic turns are evicted from the window, the next call still needs to tell verified observations apart from working hypotheses and an incomplete repair.

A structured progress record carries this state across compactions in named fields, holding active task constraints, verified observations, working hypotheses, artifact versions, and open obligations instead of replaying raw dialogue. A fixed schema lets host code flag missing fields and internal contradictions automatically, though checking whether the recorded claims match what actually happened still requires an audit against host logs. This record is a continuation manifest for the next prompt rather than an execution checkpoint: snapshotting processes, tracking dirty files on disk, and recovering in-flight tool calls belong to execution persistence.

↪ Execution checkpoints, which bind workspace snapshots and in-flight operations to a committed log position, are developed in Checkpoints.

4.6.1 Recording facts and open questions

Keeping a continuation record trustworthy starts with separating facts the host runtime can verify from hypotheses proposed by the model, so a compaction pass cannot turn an instruction like “test this repair” into a claim that “this repair passed.” Active task constraints come from the task contract rather than from a model’s summary. Artifact versions and tool outcomes come from host execution logs rather than free-form prose. A diagnosis stays marked as a hypothesis even when it explains the symptoms seen so far. In the connection-pool repair, the agent cannot loosen public API rules, widen its allowed workspace, or lift deployment blocks merely by rewriting its progress record. When an operator updates the task contract, the runtime writes the new terms directly into the prompt rather than keeping old instructions to save a prefix hit. Each progress record names the contract version it was built against, so the runtime can check its fields against current rules (table 7).

↩︎ The five-part task specification contract, the source of a task’s binding constraints, is established in The five-part task contract.

Every observation entry in table 7 follows the scope rule, so the r17 failure and the r18 pass each speak only for their own revision. Milestones record the exact check run and its result (“reproducer passed on r18”) rather than a blanket claim (“the race condition is gone”). The handle row applies the handle rule, so the agent can pull back an exact assertion diff or full trace on demand without keeping raw logs in every prompt.

4.6.2 Auditing before replacing active history

Because a model-written progress record is an untrusted candidate to replace active history, it can satisfy every schema type check (supplying a valid status string, artifact tag, and event ID) while still reporting a test run that never happened. Before swapping the record into the prompt, the runtime must therefore check both its structure and its factual claims against host logs, applying the same split between schema validity and factual correctness that governs tool calls.

↩︎ The gap between a schema-valid payload and a correct one is established for tool calls in What a grammar mask guarantees.

To avoid circular validation, the audit checks candidate records against a required-field inventory owned by the host runtime. If the runtime trusted a checklist generated by the summarizer itself, a hallucinating model could drop the no-deploy rule from both the summary and the checklist while passing self-consistency checks. Instead, the runtime reads active constraints straight from the task contract and checks artifact hashes and test outcomes against its own execution log. While that check guarantees that known contract terms and logged events are accurately reported, deciding which code excerpts the next step will need remains a selection decision that no schema check can settle automatically.

For every claimed milestone, the runtime looks up the referenced event in the execution log and compares the recorded command, exit code, artifact version, and test scope. An unlogged tool result stays marked unknown, a failed check stays failed regardless of how the summary phrases it, and a nonzero exit code records failure without proving why it failed. Open questions stay marked as hypotheses until a logged check settles them. If a candidate record claims that the full regression suite passed on r18 when the event log shows only test_waiter_notified_on_return, the audit catches the mismatch and rejects the replacement. Broader questions (whether that one test covers enough behavior, whether edge cases remain in other modules, or whether the next action is permitted) belong to test execution and policy gates rather than the summary audit.

Version checks are how the audit enforces the scope rule: a historical milestone may cite r17, whereas any claim about the current pool must point to evidence recorded at r18. Separating a missing log entry, a valid historical observation, and an unsupported claim about current code lets the runtime keep useful r17 history without mistaking it for proof that r18 works. Listing 2 puts these three checks into one host-side function, where every compared value comes from state the summarizer did not write: the runtime’s required-field inventory, the logged tool events, and the current workspace versions.

On our candidate record, Check 2 rejects the claim that the full regression suite passed on r18 because the event log records only the single reproducer test. Check 1 catches any dropped no-deployment rule or test-schedule flag, and Check 3 keeps the r17 failure as history while blocking any claim that it describes the r18 workspace. Because the audit can reject a candidate, the runtime keeps raw tool logs and source diffs in storage until the candidate record is accepted or rejected; evicting turns from the prompt never means deleting raw payloads from backing storage. When the audit finds a mismatch, the runtime can either feed the specific findings back to the model for a bounded correction pass or build a minimal record directly from its own event log. Because correction calls spend both tokens and wall-clock time, the runtime caps repair attempts, handle reads, and elapsed time rather than looping until a candidate happens to pass. If the cap is reached without a verified record, the runtime keeps a larger slice of raw history (if space permits), pulls needed files on demand, or stops with an explicit handoff error. Running out of retries never makes an unverified summary safe to admit.

4.6.3 Continuing from the record

Once the r18 progress record passes the audit, it anchors the next call without dragging along pages of refuted timeout speculation. The runtime assembles the request from the active contract, the verified record, and the fresh observations or code excerpts needed for the next step. It places them in root, trunk, and leaf order, so the audited record sits in the reusable trunk until a constraint change or new trunk refresh replaces it. The assembled request, including framing and any payloads pulled back through handles, must still fit inside \(\ContextWindow - \OutputLimit\).

In the connection-pool repair, the next call sees the r18 patch alongside the passing reproducer and the still-open API compatibility checks. Whenever a later step needs details that compaction omitted, the runtime reads the original artifacts through their handles or reports the missing evidence explicitly. Compaction acts like a reversible cache rather than a one-way loss only when two conditions hold: the raw payloads remain in storage, and each new compaction pass is built from those raw records rather than by summarizing the previous summary. Summarizing an earlier summary carries forward every earlier omission, compounding small per-round losses across the run (section 4.8.2).

Writing a structured record to disk lets summary state survive a process restart, yet a saved context record alone does not make an interrupted run resumable. Restoring workspace files, syncing git worktrees, and reconciling in-flight tool calls with unknown outcomes belong to execution recovery. Context continuation has a narrower job, ensuring that the next model call receives a checked record of task constraints and observations under their stated conditions.

↪ Restoring workspace state and reconciling in-flight tool calls on resumption are developed in Resuming a Trajectory.

Checkpoint 4.2: Continuing after compaction

A candidate progress record says the repair is complete, while the retained evidence contains only a passing reproducer on r18.

A structured progress record preserves the distinctions an agent needs after old dialogue turns are evicted, yet those recorded observations support current decisions only while the workspace still matches what they read. Whenever an edit changes a source file, updates a configuration, or modifies a test fixture, the runtime must decide which findings still apply to the present state without erasing what was observed earlier.

4.7 Context Invalidation

At the moment the repair agent’s candidate patch writes src/pool.py from r17 to r18, before the reproducer runs again, the working context still holds the r17 source excerpt, the r17 test failure, and the missed-notification hypothesis built on both. While that failure still describes how the unpatched code behaved under the earlier source, test harness, and schedule, it says nothing about whether the r18 patch fixes the bug.

The same boundary separates historical source excerpts from current code. An excerpt of src/pool.py read at r17 explains why the agent formed its missed-notification hypothesis, whereas planning another edit against r18 requires reading src/pool.py at r18. When building the next request, the runtime must choose whether to keep the r17 excerpt as labeled history, replace it with the r18 code, or drop it because the next step no longer needs it.

Definition 4.3: Context invalidation

Context invalidation withdraws a staged observation’s authority to support claims about current state whenever an underlying artifact or operational precondition changes, while preserving that observation as historical evidence of the state it originally recorded.

  1. Significance: When a superseded observation leaks into a decision prompt, it introduces an unchecked step error that compounds across multi-step execution horizons (equation 1.1). Invalidation intercepts this drift by converting stale state into an explicit refresh operation scheduled before the model acts.
  2. Distinction: Invalidation alters an observation’s applicability to the present, not its historical truth. It also differs from context deletion, which merely removes tokens from the request payload. The test failure at r17 remains an immutable, accurate record of revision r17.
  3. Common pitfall: Treating a fresh timestamp or an unexpired polling interval as a guarantee that an in-context observation matches the current artifact on disk.

When a runtime neglects context invalidation, stale observations mislead decisions in three ways. First, in offset drift, the model plans an edit against line numbers or byte offsets read at r17, so after r18 shifts the file, the patch tool either rejects the hunk (listing 3) or fuzzy-matches it into the wrong function. Second, in ghost-symptom chasing, the r17 failure trace stays unmarked in the prompt, leading the model to keep diagnosing a bug that the candidate patch already fixed. Third, in regression from old negative evidence, a long early failure log outweighs a short passing notice from a later run, prompting the model to revert or complicate a working edit. Each failure confuses historical observation with current state, a confusion that dependency tracking turns into an explicit refresh step.

4.7.1 Tracking claim dependencies

Turning those failure modes into explicit refresh steps requires recording what each entry in the prompt depends on. Test-time deliberation solved the planning version of this problem by binding each observation to what it read and propagating lost support through a candidate plan’s dependency graph (Invalidating stale and refuted evidence). Context invalidation applies the same graph machinery to the working context, with two changes. The graph now covers the observations staged in the request rather than uncommitted plan nodes, and its usual trigger is a write the agent itself ran against the workspace. Observations reached by that write are marked historical rather than retracted. In the working context, a source excerpt records its file path, repository revision, and line span or abstract syntax tree (AST) symbol, while a test run records the source commit, test-suite revision, selected test case, runtime environment, and exit status. Derived conclusions inherit dependencies from their supporting observations, so once r18 replaces r17, the behavior of the patched pool is an open question that needs fresh test runs (table 8).

The runtime maintains this dependency graph in host code rather than leaving bookkeeping to model attention. Every staged observation \(\TrajectoryObservation_{t}\) binds to its target artifact through an observation binding:

\[ \ObservationBinding(\TrajectoryObservation_{t}) = \langle \text{artifact}, \text{kind}, \text{revision}, \text{range}, \text{content id} \rangle, \tag{5}\]

where \(\ObservationBinding(\TrajectoryObservation_{t})\) records the artifact identifier, its kind (source file, test run, daemon process, or build output), the revision observed, the byte range or symbol read, and a cryptographic hash of the content. Derived entries (working hypotheses and test summaries) hold directed edges to each supporting observation, forming the in-memory dependency graph.9 Propagation follows the plan-graph traversal with one difference made possible by the binding: a tool write marks its directly bound observations historical rather than unsupported, and only the conclusions downstream of them are flagged for reassessment. Running this propagation when the patch writes r18 produces the right-hand column of table 8, while an excerpt of docs/pool.md and the API usage note built from it keep their status because nothing they depend on changed (figure 4).

9 Invalidation Is Not Retraction: A truth maintenance system (Doyle 1979) retracts a belief when its premise fails, which suits a candidate plan whose refuted diagnosis should simply disappear. An executed observation is different, because the r17 failure stays true of r17. The runtime graph therefore marks an affected observation historical, removing only its support for current-state claims, and reserves retraction for derived conclusions such as a hypothesis.

Doyle, Jon. 1979. “A Truth Maintenance System.” Artificial Intelligence 12 (3): 231–72. https://doi.org/10.1016/0004-3702(79)90008-0.

Selective invalidation is only as accurate as its recorded edges. Editing a test assertion, changing a timeout setting, or modifying a core struct immediately invalidates dependent test claims. Whenever dependency edges are coarse or incomplete, safety favors over-invalidation. Marking a wider set of entries for refresh avoids acting on silent regressions, because a missing edge never proves freshness.

Cryptographic content hashes verify whether input files stay identical across steps.10 When checking a derived result, the runtime compares the current content hashes of its direct dependencies against the hashes recorded when the tool ran. Even when all input hashes match, nondeterministic behavior (thread scheduling or network timing) means that a single passing run is still only one sample, so the manifest tracks artifact versions, execution scope, and sampling uncertainty separately.

10 Timestamps vs. Content Hashes: Make’s timestamp rule (Feldman 1979) rebuilds a target whose file was touched but not changed and can miss a real change whose timestamp moved backward. Modern build engines record cryptographic hashes of input contents and rebuild only when a dependency hash diverges (Mokhov et al. 2018). Context runtimes face the same trade-off, since a timestamp records when an inspection occurred, while a content hash confirms whether the artifact is unchanged.

Feldman, Stuart I. 1979. “Make — a Program for Maintaining Computer Programs.” Software: Practice and Experience 9 (4): 255–65. https://doi.org/10.1002/spe.4380090402.
Mokhov, Andrey, Neil Mitchell, and Simon Peyton Jones. 2018. “Build Systems à La Carte.” Proceedings of the ACM on Programming Languages 2 (ICFP): 79:1–29. https://doi.org/10.1145/3236774.

4.7.2 Detecting change and bounding uncertainty

The clearest signal that an artifact has changed is a write completed through a runtime tool. When a file-editing tool writes r18, the runtime marks all r17 source excerpts historical and records open test obligations for the modified file. Because a tool call that fails or times out can still leave partial edits on disk, the runtime checks the affected files alongside exit codes and return payloads, marking dependent observations for refresh until the on-disk state is known. All of this tracking belongs in the runtime’s dependency graph rather than in prompt reminders asking the model to remember what changed.

External processes can also change workspace files outside the agent’s tool calls. Cheap filesystem metadata (file size and modification time) offers a quick hint to re-check a file, while content hashes or git commit IDs confirm whether the bytes actually changed. Kernel filesystem watchers such as Linux’s inotify cut detection delay, yet queue overflows, unmonitored mounts, and dropped events can leave a cached view stale (Linux man-pages project 2026). Runtimes therefore treat watcher notifications as hints to re-verify hashes rather than as a complete log of changes.

Linux man-pages project. 2026. Inotify(7): Monitoring Filesystem Events.

11 Leases: Gray and Cheriton (1989) converted temporal bounds into formal consistency guarantees through leases.
