In a distributed cache, a lease grants a client exclusive or shared read rights over a datum for a fixed term, during which the server guarantees it will not permit conflicting writes until every lease expires or is explicitly released. This guarantee hinges on server-coordinated consensus. Because standard filesystems and external web services provide no such cooperative write locks to an agent runtime, a local observation TTL remains a heuristic rather than a consistency guarantee.

Gray, Cary G., and David R. Cheriton. 1989. “Leases: An Efficient Fault-Tolerant Mechanism for Distributed File Cache Consistency.” Proceedings of the Twelfth ACM Symposium on Operating Systems Principles (SOSP), 202–10. https://doi.org/10.1145/74850.74870.

To bound staleness on external resources that emit no change events, runtimes often set a time-to-live (TTL) that forces a fresh read once an observation passes a chosen age. A TTL is a practical rule of thumb tuned to how fast the environment changes and what a stale decision costs, not a guarantee of consistency.11 An observation can go stale microseconds after it is read, and an expired entry may still match an unchanged resource. Before any high-consequence action, the runtime bypasses TTL caches and reads the current state directly.

Even when the runtime refreshes context right before building a prompt, the external system can still change between prompt assembly and tool execution. Context engineering cannot close that race window inside the prompt; closing it requires action-time preconditions at the tool boundary, such as atomic compare-and-swap writes or revision checks.12 Context assembly contributes by recording the exact revision ID on which the model planned its action so the tool runner can check that revision before applying the write.

12 Optimistic Validation: In optimistic concurrency control, transactions execute reads and computations without acquiring locks, validating prior to commit that no conflicting transaction mutated their read set (Kung and Robinson 1981). If the validation check fails, the transaction aborts. Preconditions at the agent tool boundary follow the same structure. The assembled prompt supplies the version snapshot the model evaluated, and the tool runner verifies that version’s integrity before applying side effects to disk.

Kung, H. T., and John T. Robinson. 1981. “On Optimistic Methods for Concurrency Control.” ACM Transactions on Database Systems 6 (2): 213–26. https://doi.org/10.1145/319566.319567.

↪ Execution-time preconditions enforced as atomic conditional updates at the tool boundary are developed in Conditional updates.

4.7.3 Removing, replacing, or appending

Once dependency tracking or a version check marks an in-context observation outdated, the runtime must decide how to reflect that change in the next request (table 9). It can remove the outdated payload outright, replace it in place with a short marker or fresh read, or append a superseding notice at the end of the prompt. Each choice trades KV-cache prefix reuse against the risk of leaving stale tokens visible to attention.

In the connection-pool repair, an in-place marker can state directly that the earlier excerpt came from r17 and that the agent must read r18 before planning another edit. It can also keep a short note on the r17 failure if that history still informs the diagnosis. That replacement keeps the investigation coherent without presenting old code as current state, and a handle to the full r17 excerpt preserves auditability without spending working-set tokens.

Appending a notice at the end of the prompt preserves the KV-cache prefix across the turn, yet it leaves the model to weigh the earlier excerpt against the trailing correction.13 Placing the notice at the end of the request or phrasing it strongly does not guarantee that attention will favor the correction over the detailed code or error trace above it. Conversely, removing the payload drops the stale tokens from the prompt, though it can leave downstream hypotheses dangling unless the runtime also propagates the change through the dependency graph. Both removal and in-place replacement end KV-cache reuse at the first modified token, forcing the serving engine to prefill the entire suffix that follows.

13 Tombstones in Storage Engines: Log-structured storage engines like Bigtable record deletions by writing tombstones, which actively mask superseded values during read scans until major compaction discards both the tombstone and the deleted record (Chang et al. 2008). The storage engine’s read path mechanically enforces this masking. By contrast, an appended advisory notice in a language model prompt has no read filter, so the superseded text remains visible in the attention matrix, leaving the model to resolve the contradiction on its own.

Chang, Fay, Jeffrey Dean, Sanjay Ghemawat, Wilson C. Hsieh, Deborah A. Wallach, Mike Burrows, Tushar Chandra, Andrew Fikes, and Robert E. Gruber. 2008. “Bigtable: A Distributed Storage System for Structured Data.” ACM Transactions on Computer Systems 26 (2): 1–26. https://doi.org/10.1145/1365815.1365816.

Napkin Math 4.4: Token accounting for invalidation
Problem: How do payload removal, in-place replacement, and appended notices change resident token counts and the uncached suffix that the serving engine must prefill?

Variables: The baseline prompt is the 47,000-token request under the selecting policy of notebook 4.1. An outdated 850-token source excerpt from r17 sits at the start of the trunk, leaving the 15,000-token root (system instructions and tool declarations) as the unchanged prefix. An in-place tombstone marker takes 24 tokens, and an appended notice, including role and message framing, takes 128 tokens. These counts are serialized token lengths assuming no cross-boundary BPE re-tokenization. To isolate the invalidation edit itself, the leaf is held fixed from the previous call and the notice is appended at the end of the request after the leaf. Both removal and in-place replacement diverge from the previous request at the end of the root prefix, ending KV-cache reuse there.

Math: Removing the excerpt leaves 47,000 − 850 = 46,150 resident tokens, and subtracting the reusable root leaves 46,150 − 15,000 = 31,150 tokens in the uncached suffix.

Replacing the excerpt with a tombstone leaves 46,150 + 24 = 46,174 resident tokens and an uncached suffix of 46,174 − 15,000 = 31,174 tokens. Appending a notice leaves all earlier tokens untouched, giving 47,000 + 128 = 47,128 resident tokens with only the new 128-token tail beyond the cached prefix.

Systems insight: For the invalidation edit alone, appending preserves the cached prefix and prefills only 128 tokens against 31,174 under in-place replacement, roughly 244× fewer. On a real next call that also replaces the 8,000-token leaf, both choices prefill that new leaf as well, narrowing the per-call difference to the trunk that replacement recomputes. Moreover, the cache saving from appending recurs as a cost on every later call because all 850 superseded tokens remain in the prompt, diluting attention on current evidence (equation 2) and inviting ghost-symptom chasing. Runtimes therefore use the dependency graph to combine both approaches. They append a notice for historical observations that the agent cites only as background. They replace or tombstone code regions that upcoming edits target, or batch that replacement into the next scheduled trunk rewrite when the trunk prefix will be recomputed anyway (notebook 4.2). Because none of the three options loads the new code, the read of r18 and the output reserve must be budgeted separately.

Context invalidation keeps historical observations separate from claims about present state by combining dependency tracking, bounded freshness checks, and deliberate replacement rules. Whether the complete context policy actually supplies enough evidence for valid actions while lowering token spend and prefill latency is an empirical question that only end-to-end evaluation on realistic tasks can answer.

4.8 Evaluating Context Policies

At the end of the repair’s timeline, the reproducer has passed on r18, the history has been compacted, and the next request must carry the task constraints, the r17 failure of test_waiter_notified_on_return, and the r18 pass, each with its scope. At this point, the context policy succeeds only if the next prompt gives the model enough evidence to distinguish the active candidate patch from the earlier failure while showing which verification checks remain open. Token reduction alone is never evidence of success. When compaction erases version boundaries, an agent can mistake an obsolete diagnostic failure for current reality, or mistake a partial reproducer pass for complete task success, which example 4.1 dissects.

Example 4.1: A passing test without its version
Scenario: The repair stands where this chapter left it. test_waiter_notified_on_return failed at r17 and passes at r18 under the recorded configuration, and broader behavior is unverified. Before the next call, a naive compaction policy replaces these entries with one summary: “The waiter test passed; the repair is complete.” The API constraint and the deployment prohibition survive verbatim.

Diagnosis: A schema validator checking for a string-valued test_status accepts the summary without error, and a rule-based constraint check confirms that both negative constraints remain present. Neither check, however, detects that a single-case observation was turned into a claim of complete task success. An evidence probe closes this gap by freezing the history and inspecting both the assembled prompt and the model’s next decision, checking candidate and test identities, reproducer scope, and open acceptance checks. In a controlled variant, the evaluator edits a relevant test or source file after the passing run. A sound policy leads the agent to see that the earlier pass no longer covers the modified code, and to run fresh checks before declaring the repair done. An edit to an unrelated file serves as a negative control to confirm that the policy does not invalidate context indiscriminately.

Systems lesson: A context policy must preserve causal relationships and version bindings, not merely isolated field names. A test result has meaning only relative to the exact artifact and execution conditions under which it was produced, and subsequent decisions must respect those boundaries. Targeted evidence probes pinpoint semantic information loss, while repeated dynamic trials verify whether the agent acts on those preserved distinctions during execution.

Every mechanism in this chapter meets at failures of this kind: selection decides what enters the prompt, staging sets representation and zone order, compaction and progress records govern what survives across trunk rewrites, and invalidation decides when stale support must be refreshed. Judging a policy by summary length alone misses the failures that happen at those joins, so evaluation must test the complete context policy (external reads, schema checks, and fallback paths included) under the target workload’s rules and budgets.

4.8.1 Comparing complete policies

Testing the complete policy means accounting for every read, summary call, and retry it triggers outside the main prompt, because comparing main prompt lengths alone can credit a “compact” policy that quietly shifts token spend into extra tool reads or helper calls. A fair comparison starts every repair trial from the same source snapshot, task contract, and initial observations, and charges every auxiliary read or model call to the policy that issued it. The harness holds the model, sampling settings, tool definitions, and acceptance suite fixed while varying only the context policy under shared ceilings such as the 128,000-token window and 30-turn step budget.

A complete policy specification must also state the operating rules that would otherwise hide costs or failure paths. A sliding-window policy must state how it pins system instructions and task constraints. A summarizing policy must state its trigger and target watermarks, protected-field inventory, and fallback path when a summary fails the audit. Any policy that moves raw payloads behind handles must state what happens when a handle read fails. Labels like “sliding window,” “summarizer,” or “structured record” leave out these rules, even though fallback behavior often decides whether an agent recovers or fails when context is missing.

Repeated runs across task cohorts remain necessary even under greedy decoding with fixed seeds, because distributed serving engines, kernel nondeterminism, and asynchronous tool scheduling all introduce run-to-run variation. Evaluation logs must record model versions, serving-engine builds, sampling settings, sandbox images, and KV-cache policies so that infrastructure changes are not mistaken for context-policy gains. A single passing run shows only that a trajectory can succeed once, not how reliably the policy performs.

↩︎ Why greedy decoding with a fixed seed still varies across served calls, and the difference between replaying a trajectory and re-querying the model, are established in Repetition and replay.

↪ How many tasks and repeated runs an estimate needs, and the confidence intervals on acceptance rates, are developed in Statistical Evaluation Rigor.

4.8.2 Measuring retention, behavior, and cost

A failed trial may come from a compaction pass that dropped a required constraint, or from a model that ignored a constraint still in the prompt. To tell them apart, the evaluation harness defines a required-information inventory against the task fixture before inspecting any runs. For the connection-pool repair, that inventory lists the public API rules, the approved workspace path, the no-deploy rule, the source and test revisions, and the scope of each test run.

Required-field retention is the fraction of required items whose value, scope, and provenance match the ground-truth fixture; merely keeping a field named test_status does not count. Exact identifiers and compiler messages can be checked by string equality, whereas paraphrased claims need semantic checks that account for grading uncertainty. Recording omitted, altered, and unsupported fields separately tells engineers whether the failure came from aggressive pruning or from hallucinated rewriting.

Retention across repeated compaction passes is much stricter than retention across a single pass, because an item can drop out during an intermediate rewrite and only reappear later through an expensive re-read. The benchmark must therefore state whether it checks retention at every compaction boundary, uninterrupted survival across the whole run, or presence just before a decision step. In equation 6, \(\TaskContract\) is the set of critical constraints from the task contract (the negative scope of the goal and the limits on permitted actions) rather than the five-part contract tuple itself. The quantity \(|\TaskContract|\) is their count, and \(\CompactionBoundaryCount\) counts compaction boundaries rather than turns or retries. Multiplying the binary indicator across all \(\CompactionBoundaryCount\) boundaries means that a single drop at any intermediate boundary sets that constraint’s product to zero for the rest of the run, even if a later read restores the text. For uninterrupted retention across \(\CompactionBoundaryCount\) compaction boundaries, the retention fraction is

\[ \ConstraintRetentionFraction(\CompactionBoundaryCount) = \frac{1}{|\TaskContract|} \sum_{\CriticalConstraint \in \TaskContract} \prod_{i=1}^{\CompactionBoundaryCount} \IndicatorFunction\left[\CriticalConstraint \text{ correctly retained at boundary } \CompactionBoundaryIndex\right]. \tag{6}\]

A small per-round loss compounds geometrically over repeated compactions.

The product in equation 6 shows why summarizing an earlier summary is so hazardous. When a required constraint survives each compaction round independently with probability \(1-\CompactionLossProbability\), its survival probability across \(\CompactionBoundaryCount\) rounds compounds to \((1-\CompactionLossProbability)^\CompactionBoundaryCount\), following the same compounding law as equation 1.1 with compaction passes in place of decision steps. Under a seemingly small per-round loss rate of 5 percent, a constraint survives 10 compaction rounds with probability 59.9 percent, but survives 40 rounds with probability only 12.9 percent. That rapid decay is why production runtimes check protected fields in host code at every compaction boundary rather than trusting an unconstrained model summary. Extending the calculation to the full working set requires joint survival estimates because real compaction losses often correlate across fields within the same pass.

Behavioral metrics likewise need explicit denominators. A constraint-violation rate counts violating actions against the decision points where that constraint applied, logging blocked proposals separately from executed violations so generation errors are not confused with missing host gates. In the same way, the stale-support rate

\[ \StaleSupportRate = \frac{\text{decisions that treat superseded support as current}}{\text{decisions that require current evidence}} \tag{7}\]

restricts its denominator to decisions where evidence freshness affects validity. Under the scope rule, citing the r17 failure as history after moving to r18 is valid, whereas citing it as proof of how r18 behaves is a stale-support error.

End-to-end task acceptance estimates the acceptance probability \(\AcceptanceProbability\) in equation 1.2:

\[ \EstimatedAcceptanceProbability = \frac{\AcceptedTasks}{\EvaluationTrialCount}, \tag{8}\]

where \(\AcceptedTasks\) counts the trials that pass the full verification standard within their resource budgets and \(\EvaluationTrialCount\) counts all assigned trials. Passing the single-case reproducer is only an intermediate milestone, and timed-out runs, tool crashes, or context overflows must stay in the denominator \(\EvaluationTrialCount\) rather than being dropped. Table 10 brings these representation, behavioral, and outcome metrics together under one measurement contract.

↪ The task acceptance contract that decides which trials count as accepted is developed in The Task Acceptance Contract.

Compression measures representation size rather than decision quality. To compute compression at a decision step, serialize both an uncompacted reference prompt containing the full raw history and the candidate prompt produced by the context policy with the same tokenizer, including system instructions, tool declarations, schemas, and framing delimiters in both. The request compression ratio is

\[ \RequestCompressionRatio = \frac{\ReferenceRequestLength}{\PolicyRequestLength}, \tag{9}\]

where \(\ReferenceRequestLength\) and \(\PolicyRequestLength\) are the token counts of the uncompacted reference request and the policy-generated request. Because \(\ReferenceRequestLength\) is an analytical baseline, it can exceed the serving engine’s context window without needing to be run. Values of equation 9 above \(1.0\) indicate token savings, provided both numerator and denominator count complete serialized requests rather than comparing raw observations against a framed prompt.

Compression ratios also depend on how a run unfolds. On fixed-trace probes where every policy processes the same recorded transcript, request lengths compare directly. In live closed-loop trials, however, a high compression ratio can mean only that a weak policy wandered through extra exploratory turns that then had to be compacted. Reports should therefore pair compression ratios with total token counts and full system spend (including summarization calls, search queries, and handle reads) so a policy cannot look cheap merely by moving tokens out of the main prompt into helper calls.

Checking that an agent actually uses what the prompt retains requires causal interventions rather than static inspection, because token presence, attention weights, or copied identifiers show only correlation. By removing or altering a key variable in a controlled fixture (such as the branch tag or test status) while holding the rest of the prompt fixed, an evaluator can see whether the model’s next tool call changes accordingly. If the call stays the same, the model is ignoring that field under the current layout. Those static interventions still need to be paired with live task runs, where an agent can recover from an omitted prompt field by reading the workspace through a tool call. Counterfactual probes test omitted payloads in the same way. If a compaction policy replaces raw test logs with an external handle, temporarily disabling the handle store shows whether the agent falls back cleanly or guesses when retrieval fails. Field-presence checks never see this failure mode.

Choosing a production context policy means weighing all of these measurements together across task acceptance, failure modes, latency, and total dollar cost under shared limits. A structured-record policy that keeps every schema field yet still leads the model to declare victory on an incomplete check fails its purpose. The right production policy is the one that minimizes the effective cost per accepted task, \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\), across the evaluation cohort: shrinking the prompt lowers per-call spend, but if aggressive pruning lowers \(\AcceptanceProbability\), the cost per accepted task rises sharply.

Checkpoint 4.3: Testing what survives compaction

A candidate policy preserves the manifest’s field names but drops specific values and version relationships. Its resulting request is measurably shorter than the uncompacted reference prompt.

Every evaluation result is only as strong as its test fixtures, evidence rules, and cost accounting. The most common evaluation mistakes come from treating a single intermediate metric as proof of overall quality, such as mistaking a shorter prompt for better context or mistaking safety instructions in the prompt for an enforced execution gate.

4.9 Fallacies and Pitfalls

A prompt can be compressed until it drops a required constraint, or left as an uncurated transcript where superseded tool outputs sit beside current state. Both failures happen when engineers ask the prompt payload to do the work of state tracking, durable storage, and execution enforcement all at once.

Fallacy: If every token fits, every needed fact is usable.

Admitting a prompt within the interface’s context window does not ensure that the model can attend to and extract every critical fact (section 4.3). Token capacity sets only an upper bound on input size, whereas evidence retrieval depends on how attention weight is distributed. For example, under fixed logits, the toy attention head in equation 2 assigns 96.8 percent of its attention weight to the target token across a sequence of 100 keys. This share declines to 2.9 percent when distributed across 100,000 keys, even though all 100,000 keys fit within the context limit. Validating a context layout therefore requires varying information density and placement alongside sequence length, measuring actual task completion on realistic workloads rather than relying on raw token budgets.

Pitfall: Treating deterministic extraction as lossless task understanding.

Deterministic filters and parsers guarantee reproducibility, but reproducibility is not task understanding. A heuristic parser consistently extracts the same error lines while silently dropping the warning that preceded them. For example, filtering exclusively for FAILED status lines in listing 1 isolates the failing assertion while discarding the event sequence that makes the failure actionable. Deterministic pipelines make transformations inspectable and debuggable, yet they remain vulnerable to silent data loss. Reliable runtimes guard against this by marking protected fields that summarization passes cannot discard, noting omitted spans, and retaining content-addressed handles to raw payloads so that an agent can re-read original data when needed.

Fallacy: A valid progress-record schema guarantees continued compliance.

A structured schema verifies syntactic presence, but it cannot guarantee that task constraints survive repeated compaction cycles. Under the hypothetical 5 percent per-round loss rate modeled in equation 6, an operational policy constraint survives 40 iterative compaction steps with only 12.9 percent probability, even though every intermediate serialized record validates cleanly against the schema. Systems engineers must treat prompt context as an advisory channel rather than an enforcement layer. Runtimes must validate protected fields against ground-truth sources at every compaction boundary (section 4.6), check subsequent agent actions against security policies, and enforce hard execution boundaries in host code rather than relying on in-context reminders.

Pitfall: Keeping obsolete support solely to preserve a prefix hit.

Appending corrections to the end of a transcript preserves prefix-cache hits for the next call. In notebook 4.4, the invalidation edit alone makes in-place replacement reprocess roughly 244× as many tokens beyond the prefix, a gap that narrows to the size of the trunk once the next call’s new leaf is counted. However, prioritizing short-term cache hits retains 850 superseded tokens across all subsequent requests, forcing the model to repeatedly reconcile stale text with appended errata. The dependency graph decides which rule applies. Evidence in any region that a later edit or current-state decision targets should be replaced, and appended notices reserved for inert history that the agent cites only as rationale (section 4.7).

Fallacy: A larger context window removes the need to select and compact.

Expanding context windows raises physical token limits, but it does not eliminate the compounding latency, cost, and attention dilution of unmanaged histories. As demonstrated in notebook 4.1, an append-only transcript policy running across a 30-turn horizon requires 167,000 tokens by its final request. While an expanded window accommodates this trajectory, it bills 3,030,000 input tokens compared to 1,410,000 tokens for a selective policy that maintains a compact working set of 8,000 observation tokens, a factor of 2.1× in token volume. The extra tokens also dilute attention over the evidence required for subsequent tool calls. Structured selection and compaction remain necessary, reducing both the effective trial cost \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\) and the distraction within the request regardless of window capacity.

Pitfall: Placing unnecessary volatile fields before reusable content.

Prefix-caching engines match sequence tokens strictly from left to right. Inserting dynamic values (timestamps, request identifiers, ephemeral counters) near the start of a prompt breaks cache reuse across turns, even when the system prompt, schemas, and task instructions that follow are completely unchanged. In notebook 4.2, this volatile layout increases the input bill so that it is 3.2× that of the stable layout under explicit reuse and pricing assumptions. Exact-prefix matching halts at the very first differing token, regardless of message roles or field meaning. System architects should structure prompts hierarchically, pinning invariant instructions and tools at the root prefix and deferring volatile, per-call parameters to the leaves.

These pitfalls share one root cause: treating the prompt window as a single store for evidence, task state, and execution enforcement. Sound context engineering divides those jobs across the runtime, using the working context to stage task-relevant evidence for the next decision, external stores to hold verified history, and host gates to enforce action boundaries.

4.10 Summary

Fitting an execution trace within a model’s context window does not ensure that the model will make a sound decision. Throughout this chapter, the repair agent’s most instructive failures rarely stemmed from context window exhaustion. Instead, they occurred when the context representation severed causal relationships. A discarded hypothesis remained displayed alongside contradictory evidence, and a passing reproducer lost its revision metadata. An automated summary silently dropped an operational constraint, and a cached code excerpt persisted after the underlying file had changed. Each case reflects a failure of context state management rather than token capacity. Solving these breakdowns requires runtime bookkeeping outside the model (explicit working-set management, dependency tracking, cache-aware layout, and artifact versioning), not a larger context window or more elaborate prompt wording. Table 11 summarizes the quantitative relations developed throughout this chapter to budget context tokens, schedule compaction passes, invalidate stale observations, and evaluate end-to-end task efficiency.

Key Takeaways: Context is selected information for the next decision
  • Context budgets require explicit working-set management: Every serialized request must preserve sufficient headroom for generation, but staying within token limits does not guarantee that the prompt supplies the necessary evidence. Naively appending every tool observation quickly exhausts the context window. In the reference repair agent, uncontrolled logging overflows the context window at turn 21 of 30. Effective runtimes actively bound and select per-turn observations to match the execution horizon while measuring decision quality directly.
  • Prefix stability governs cache reuse rather than semantic authority: Placing static instructions and tool definitions in a stable prefix eliminates redundant prefill computation through KV-cache reuse. Across the repair trajectory, under the chapter’s stated reuse and price assumptions, an unstable layout’s input bill came to 3.2× that of a cache-aligned layout. Cache layout, however, should never be confused with authority. Message roles, cache boundaries, and data provenance are separate architectural concerns, and staging content in a privileged prefix does not validate untrusted tool outputs.
  • Compaction mechanisms must enforce strict schema retention: Truncation and summarization inevitably discard detail. Because small per-round losses compound geometrically over long trajectories, compaction policies must explicitly safeguard the exact fields required by downstream tools and decisions. Runtimes should validate generated summaries against their original records at every compaction boundary, retaining raw-payload handles so the agent can deterministically retrieve omitted evidence on demand.
  • Observation validity must be bound to artifact versions: Every tool observation reflects the specific system environment and artifact revision present when the tool ran. When an agent modifies source code or configuration files, earlier test results cease to validate claims about the current state, even though they remain historically accurate records of prior turns. Runtime managers must track revisions explicitly, invalidating stale claims while preserving observations that remain tied to untouched components.
  • Context policies must be evaluated on end-to-end task outcomes: Intermediate metrics such as compression ratio, token count, and field retention provide useful diagnostics, but they do not measure decision quality. A compaction or retrieval strategy improves a system only if it reduces the effective cost per accepted task completion, accounting for the overhead of compaction calls, retries, and failed trajectories across all evaluated trials.

Treating prompt context as an active working set (principle 4) turns context engineering from ad hoc prompt assembly into a repeatable systems discipline. Rather than assuming that a larger window will resolve ambiguity, engineers must verify whether an assembled prompt contains the dependencies needed for the next decision step. Scoping observations by artifact revision reinforces the rule that authority remains with the external source of truth (principle 6), enabling the runtime to invalidate superseded evidence without corrupting historical audit logs. Working together, these principles ensure that context preparation is transparent about its cache costs, accountable for the evidence it provides, and explicit about unresolved dependencies.

4.11 Historical Perspective and Further Reading

Managing bounded fast memory against a much larger backing store is one of the foundational challenges of computer systems. When virtual memory decoupled address spaces from physical DRAM, Belady established the theoretical ceiling for page replacement by comparing online policies against an optimal offline algorithm with clairvoyant knowledge of future memory accesses (Belady 1966). Denning grounded this theory in measurable program behavior with the working-set model (Denning 1968), defining an empirical working set over a sliding time window to prevent thrashing. Context engineering confronts a similar capacity limit, with one sharp systems difference. Unlike hardware with memory-management units, a transformer forward pass emits no access traces or page faults, so the runtime cannot passively observe token reuse and must instead predict and assemble the relevant working set before issuing each model call. Virtual context systems such as MemGPT (Packer et al. 2023) adopted this hierarchical model directly, paging structured dialogue and retrieved facts between the active context window and external storage.

Packer, Charles, Vivian Fang, Shishir G. Patil, Kevin Lin, Sarah Wooders, and Joseph E. Gonzalez. 2023. “MemGPT: Towards LLMs as Operating Systems.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2310.08560, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2310.08560.
O’Neil, Patrick, Edward Cheng, Dieter Gawlick, and Elizabeth O’Neil. 1996. “The Log-Structured Merge-Tree (LSM-Tree).” Acta Informatica 33 (4): 351–85.

The mechanisms required to maintain consistency across derived agent state draw from decades of research in automated reasoning, software build systems, and distributed storage. Classical truth maintenance systems established early formalisms for tracking dependencies. Doyle (Doyle 1979) recorded explicit justifications for each maintained belief, automatically propagating invalidations whenever an underlying premise collapsed. Software build tools addressed the same synchronization challenge at the filesystem boundary. Feldman’s make (Feldman 1979) reconciled targets with dependencies using file modification timestamps, a strategy that modern build architectures subsequently generalized by tracking cryptographic content hashes and execution traces (Mokhov et al. 2018). In distributed systems and databases, optimistic concurrency control (Kung and Robinson 1981) protected state validity by verifying at commit time that underlying read sets remained unaltered, while cache leases (Gray and Cheriton 1989) bound staleness through time-limited writer contracts. Similarly, data provenance research clarified how derived views inherit lineage from source records (Buneman et al. 2001), and log-structured storage engines established how to compact and tombstone superseded records without stalling active reads (O’Neil et al. 1996; Chang et al. 2008). Agent runtimes encounter the same dilemma, because intermediate summaries and cached observations outlive their inputs unless governed by explicit dependency tracking and proactive eviction.

The foundation model layer introduced its own distinct set of systems bottlenecks. Transformer attention (Vaswani et al. 2017) structured every input sequence as an all-to-all competition for weight, making sequence length a quadratic compute and linear memory cost during inference. Retrieval-augmented generation (Lewis et al. 2020) brought dynamic document chunks into this input space, while reasoning-and-acting frameworks such as ReAct (Yao et al. 2023) established the now-standard agent loop of interleaved reasoning, tool invocation, and environment observations. As multi-turn interaction traces expanded, empirical evaluation began to distinguish nominal context capacity from usable context quality. Liu et al. (2024) documented the U-shaped “lost-in-the-middle” phenomenon, showing that retrieval performance falls when critical evidence sits in the interior of a long prompt. Shi et al. (2023) demonstrated that even benign, irrelevant context distracts model attention and degrades task accuracy, while Hsieh et al. (2024) revealed that basic needle-in-a-haystack benchmarks substantially overestimate real-world usable context on complex multi-hop queries. At the serving layer, systems such as vLLM (Kwon et al. 2023) and SGLang (Zheng et al. 2024) introduced paged attention and prefix caching to share key-value tensors across requests, turning prompt structure into a primary determinant of accelerator memory footprint and time-to-first-token latency. Finally, because naive history compaction can distort the evidence it keeps, findings by Maynez et al. (2020) on widespread hallucination in abstractive summaries show why runtime compaction requires verifiable provenance audits.

Vaswani, Ashish, Noam Shazeer, Niki Parmar, Jakob Uszkoreit, Llion Jones, Aidan N. Gomez, Lukasz Kaiser, and Illia Polosukhin. 2017. “Attention Is All You Need.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 30: 5998–6008.
Lewis, Patrick, Ethan Perez, Aleksandra Piktus, Fabio Petroni, Vladimir Karpukhin, Naman Goyal, Heinrich Küttler, et al. 2020. “Retrieval-Augmented Generation for Knowledge-Intensive NLP Tasks.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems 33: 9459–74.
Kwon, Woosuk, Zhuohan Li, Siyuan Zhuang, Ying Sheng, Lianmin Zheng, Cody Hao Yu, Joseph Gonzalez, Hao Zhang, and Ion Stoica. 2023. “Efficient Memory Management for Large Language Model Serving with PagedAttention.” Proceedings of the 29th Symposium on Operating Systems Principles, 611–26. https://doi.org/10.1145/3600006.3613165.
  • Denning (1968) introduces the classical working-set model based on temporal locality, illustrating both how the working set maps to context management and why the lack of observable memory reference strings in model inference requires proactive curation.
  • Doyle (1979) establishes the foundational treatment of truth maintenance systems, formalizing how explicit dependency records enable non-monotonic invalidation whenever underlying assumptions change.
  • Mokhov et al. (2018) formalize build systems by cleanly separating staleness detection from execution scheduling, providing a principled blueprint for decoupling dependency invalidation from prompt reconstruction in agent runtimes.
  • Liu et al. (2024) isolate prompt position effects to demonstrate that transformer attention degrades when target information appears in the middle of long sequences, establishing an experimental methodology for evaluating context placement on realistic workloads.
  • Hsieh et al. (2024) demonstrate why isolated needle-in-a-haystack tests overestimate usable context windows, offering a comprehensive benchmarking framework to evaluate multi-hop tracing, aggregation, and variable tracking across expanded context lengths.
  • Maynez et al. (2020) quantify factual unfaithfulness and hallucination in abstractive summarization, providing direct empirical motivation for rigorous provenance tracking and audit mechanisms when compacting agent interaction histories.
Denning, Peter J. 1968. “The Working Set Model for Program Behavior.” Communications of the ACM 11 (5): 323–33. https://doi.org/10.1145/363095.363141.
Liu, Nelson F., Kevin Lin, John Hewitt, Ashwin Paranjape, Michele Bevilacqua, Fabio Petroni, and Percy Liang. 2024. “Lost in the Middle: How Language Models Use Long Contexts.” Transactions of the Association for Computational Linguistics 12: 157–73. https://doi.org/10.1162/tacl_a_00638.

4.12 Exercises

Unless a problem states otherwise, use the reference repair harness and the hypothetical prices in table 12. Token counts are for complete serialized requests, prices are problem inputs rather than any provider’s schedule, and a reused prefix is assumed to have compatible cached state available.

1. Tool catalogs and the output reserve (Basic). The repair harness gains a larger tool catalog and longer patch outputs. Its tool declarations grow to 30,000 tokens, and the runtime raises \(\OutputLimit\) to 16,000 tokens. Application instructions stay at 3,000 tokens and task state at 24,000, and every tool result is appended at 4,000 tokens.

  1. Using equation 1, compute the input limit, the observation budget, the number of appended turns that fit, and the largest per-turn digest that carries the full step budget \(\TurnHorizon\).
  2. A proposal loads tool declarations on demand, the on-demand staging that Static and on-demand staging weighs against static staging, and keeps only 6,000 tokens of core declarations resident. Repeat (a).
  3. Show that no tool-declaration policy lets unmodified 4,000-token results be appended for all \(\TurnHorizon\) turns under the 16,000-token reserve. Which term must the design change instead? Compare the per-turn digest each declaration policy allows, and state what the on-demand policy adds to a request whenever a turn needs a non-core tool.

2. When appending stops being cheaper (Basic). Every request carries a fixed 39,000 tokens of instructions, declarations, and task state. The appending policy adds \(\TurnAppendLength = 4{,}000\) tokens of tool results per turn and keeps them all; the selecting policy keeps \(\ObservationTokenCount = 8{,}000\) tokens of observations on every call. Assume no prefix reuse and a window large enough to admit every request.

  1. Write the cumulative input tokens of each policy over \(\TurnHorizon\) turns. At which call does the appending policy’s single request first exceed the selecting policy’s, and at which horizon does its cumulative bill first exceed?
  2. A longer task runs a 60-turn step budget. Compute both cumulative bills in tokens and at the uncached input price, and their ratio.
  3. Show that the ratio of the two bills is exactly linear in \(\TurnHorizon\) and find its slope. At what horizon does appending cost three times as much as selecting? Use the result to critique a plan to “add selection later, once tasks get long.”

3. Look-alike evidence versus bulk (Basic). In the toy head of equation 2, the target key has logit \(\TargetKeyLogit = 6\). The request holds 40,000 unrelated keys at logit \(-2\) and a superseded 850-token r17 excerpt whose keys share identifiers with the evidence, giving each a logit of 2. All logits stay fixed as keys are added or removed.

  1. Compute \(\TargetKeyAttentionWeight\) without the excerpt and with it.
  2. How many unrelated keys contribute as much to the normalizer as the 850 excerpt keys?
  3. The runtime can either drop half of the unrelated keys or drop the excerpt. Compute \(\TargetKeyAttentionWeight\) for each choice, and for dropping every unrelated key while keeping the excerpt. Which removal should the runtime make first, and which measurement from section 4.8 would confirm the choice on a trained model?

4. Choosing a watermark gap (Intermediate). A noisier test suite raises arrivals to \(\TurnAppendLength = 6{,}000\) tokens. Compaction returns the request to \(\CompactionTargetLength = 47{,}000\) tokens: a 15,000-token root, a 24,000-token trunk, and an 8,000-token leaf. The output reserve is \(\OutputLimit = 8{,}000\). Two triggers are proposed, \(\CompactionTriggerLength = 92{,}000\) and \(\CompactionTriggerLength = 68{,}000\). Model each cycle as \(\CompactionCycleTurnCount\) ordinary calls at request lengths \(\CompactionTargetLength + \CompactionCycleTurnIndex \, \TurnAppendLength\) for \(\CompactionCycleTurnIndex = 1, \ldots, \CompactionCycleTurnCount\), the last of which triggers a compaction pass over the same request. The first ordinary call after a pass reuses only the root, because the pass rewrote the trunk; every later call reuses the whole previous request. Each ordinary call generates 1,000 output tokens. The compaction pass processes its entire input uncached and generates a 6,000-token record.

  1. For each trigger, compute \(\CompactionCycleTurnCount\) from equation 3, the trigger length and overshoot, whether the trigger request plus \(\OutputLimit\) fits the window, and the number of passes within \(\TurnHorizon\).
  2. Compute \(\CompactionPassCost\), the mean ordinary-call cost, and \(\AmortizedTurnCost\) from equation 4 for each trigger.
  3. Each pass independently loses each of 10 unprotected constraints with probability 0.02. Compute the probability that all 10 survive every pass of the step budget under each trigger.
  4. Choose a trigger and explain why the narrow gap loses on both cost and retention even though each of its passes is cheaper. What measured property of the workload would justify the narrow gap anyway?

5. Replace now, or ride the next rewrite? (Intermediate). Start from the request of notebook 4.4: 47,000 tokens behind a 15,000-token root, with an 850-token r17 excerpt opening the trunk, a 24-token replacement marker, and a 128-token appended notice. Twenty calls remain, and each later call reuses the whole previous request; the new leaf content is the same under both policies and can be ignored.

  1. Compute the input cost of the next call under replacement and under an appended notice.
  2. How many more tokens does the appended policy keep resident on every later call, what do they cost per call at the reused-prefix price, and after how many calls would the two policies break even on price alone?
  3. A compaction pass is scheduled six calls from now and will rewrite the trunk regardless. Propose an invalidation policy that uses this fact, and state the condition under which it must replace the excerpt immediately anyway. Using equation 7, identify which of the next six decisions belong in the denominator and how the policy keeps the numerator at zero.

6. Paying for a protected-field check (Intermediate). A model-written progress record loses each of 12 required constraints independently with probability \(\CompactionLossProbability = 0.03\) per compaction round, and the trajectory runs 3 rounds. A lost constraint makes the task unacceptable; with every constraint intact, \(\AcceptanceProbability = 0.6\). An attempt costs $2. A protected-field check in the style of listing 2 detects 90 percent of losses and repairs each detected loss; budget it as one $0.05 correction call per round.

  1. Without the check, compute the probability that all 12 constraints survive, the resulting \(\AcceptanceProbability\), and \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\).
  2. Repeat with the check, treating undetected losses as the new per-round loss.
  3. What per-round loss would an unchecked summarizer need to keep all-constraint survival at or above 95 percent over 3 rounds?
  4. With the check in place, after how many rounds does all-constraint survival first fall below 50 percent? The runtime in section 4.6 copies contract constraints directly from the task contract. What does that change, and which protected fields remain exposed to \(\CompactionLossProbability\)?

7. A stable root for short sessions (Advanced). The support agent serves 50,000 sessions a day. Each request carries a 20,000-token root of policy instructions and tool declarations and a 2,000-token customer record. A session makes 6 calls; before call \(\TurnIndex\) the request has grown by 1,500 tokens per turn to \(22{,}000 + 1{,}500\,\TurnIndex\) tokens, and each call generates 300 output tokens. Sessions arrive often enough that compatible root state is always resident. Layout A places the customer record and a session timestamp ahead of the root; Layout B places the root first. Within a session, every call after the first reuses the whole previous request under either layout.

  1. Compute the input and output cost per session, the daily bill, and the token-weighted prefix hit rate under each layout.
  2. Show that the entire difference arises on the first call of each session. Which term of equation 1 binds here, and how does that differ from what binds the repair agent over its step budget?
  3. A developer templates the customer’s name into the first line of the policy instructions to personalize the tone. Which layout does that recreate, and what does it cost per day?
  4. A cost review proposes cutting the root to 10,000 tokens by removing policy text. Under Layout B, compute the saving per session. With a 90 percent resolution rate at the full root, and each unresolved session escalating to a human at $4, how many percentage points of resolution can the cut lose before it costs more than it saves? What evidence should the proposal present before it ships?

8. Freshness through a human approval (Advanced). The remediation agent decides on a production change from 5 observations of shared state. Other actors change each observed resource independently, as a Poisson process with mean rate \(\ResourceChangeRate\) of one change per 20 minutes. The runtime refreshes an observation only when it is older than a 10-minute time-to-live, so at decision time each observation’s age is uniformly distributed between 0 and 10 minutes.

  1. Compute the probability that one observation still describes its resource at decision time and the probability that all 5 do. If every decision requires current evidence for all 5, estimate \(\StaleSupportRate\) from equation 7.
  2. A teammate proposes refreshing all 5 observations immediately before each model call. The call takes 30 seconds, and the action executes after a human approval that takes 10 minutes. Compute the probability that at least one of the 5 resources changes between refresh and execution, first ignoring the approval and then including it. What does the result show about the limit of any context-refresh policy for this workload?
  3. Only one observation, the configuration version the change edits, determines whether the action is safe; the other four were diagnostic. Redesign the policy around the binding \(\ObservationBinding(\TrajectoryObservation_{t})\) of equation 5 and an action-time precondition at the tool boundary that re-observes and re-requests approval when the precondition fails. Compute the probability that the precondition holds at execution and the expected number of approval rounds, and compare with a precondition on all 5 observations. Contrast the change signal available here with the one the repair agent uses after its own patch.

What’s Next: From selected tokens to physical attention state
The runtime has chosen the token sequence for the next call, and the prefix-layout calculation priced its reused prefix as a discounted line on the bill. Behind that discount sits physical state. The serving system must decide whether to retain the attention state computed from earlier compatible prefixes, and holding it occupies accelerator memory even while the agent generates nothing and waits on a tool, memory that competing requests could use. KV Cache Management sizes that state and develops the storage, sharing, and retention decisions for it. Those decisions can change execution cost without changing which observations belong in the logical working context.
